### Issue-First Hypothesizer — Round 1

 **Root cause:** The file writing in `persist_tree` uses an auto-detected encoding that may not support Unicode characters (e.g., 'charmap'/cp1252 on Windows), causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated/wiped because Python's `open()` with 'w' mode truncates the file before writing.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Ensure files are always written with UTF-8 encoding (which supports all Unicode characters), regardless of what encoding was detected for reading. Alternatively, catch encoding errors during write and retry with UTF-8, or use a safer write pattern (write to temp file, then rename).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error message `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` indicates the file is being written with Windows-1252 (charmap) encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` which comes from `get_encoding()` auto-detection
3. The file gets wiped because Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately, and if the `f.write()` fails due to encoding error, the file remains empty
4. The dbt templater passes encoding through the chain, but the core issue is the write encoding doesn't match the content's actual character set

**Weaknesses:**
- I didn't find existing tests for Unicode handling in the fix path
- The exact path from dbt templater through to persist_tree could have other encoding handoff issues I didn't fully trace
- There may be legitimate reasons to preserve the original file encoding that I'm not considering

**Confidence:** High. The 'charmap' codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue when using 'w' mode.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Stack Trace Tracer's focus on encoding detection, but I believe the fix should be at the write point (always use UTF-8 for writing) rather than improving detection
- The issue is not just detection but the mismatch between detected encoding and the actual character content - UTF-8 is the safest write encoding since it supports all Unicode characters
- The file wiping is a secondary effect of the encoding error during write, not a separate bug

### Minimal Diff Finder — Round 1

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method in `linted_file.py` opens the file with mode "w" (which truncates immediately) and uses an auto-detected encoding that may not support Unicode characters, causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file empty when the write fails.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding

**Proposed fix direction:** Change the file write operation to use UTF-8 encoding explicitly (since it supports all Unicode characters), or implement atomic file writing (write to a temporary file first, then rename on success).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` indicates the write is using Windows-1252 encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` from auto-detection
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails, the file remains empty
4. The `get_encoding()` function in `file_helpers.py` uses chardet which may return "ascii" or "windows-1252" for files with limited character samples, even if they contain UTF-8 characters

**Weaknesses:**
- I haven't verified if there are legitimate use cases where preserving the original file encoding is important
- The dbt templater path may have additional encoding complications I didn't fully trace
- There may be other places in the codebase with similar file writing patterns

**Confidence:** High. The "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Issue-First Hypothesizer's analysis of the root cause and location
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater is relevant but secondary - the core issue is in the file writing logic in `linted_file.py`
- The minimal fix is to use UTF-8 for writing, which will handle all Unicode characters correctly regardless of what encoding was detected for reading

### Issue-First Hypothesizer — Round 2

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method in `linted_file.py` uses an auto-detected file encoding (via chardet) when writing files, which may return a limited encoding like Windows-1252 ("charmap") that cannot encode Unicode characters like '→', causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated/wiped because Python's `open()` with mode "w" truncates the file before writing.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Always use UTF-8 encoding when writing files in `persist_tree`, since UTF-8 supports all Unicode characters. The encoding used for reading can remain auto-detected, but writing should use a superset encoding (UTF-8) to ensure all characters can be persisted safely. Alternatively, implement atomic file writing (write to temp file, then rename on success).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error message `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` confirms the write is using Windows-1252 encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` which originates from `get_encoding()` auto-detection in `file_helpers.py`
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails with an encoding error, the file remains empty (explaining the "wiped" file symptom)
4. The `get_encoding()` function uses chardet which can return "ascii" or "windows-1252" for files with limited character samples, even if they contain UTF-8 characters

**Weaknesses:**
- I didn't trace the complete encoding flow through the dbt templater to confirm there aren't additional encoding issues there
- There may be legitimate use cases where preserving the original file encoding is important (though UTF-8 is a superset of ASCII and most common encodings)
- I haven't verified if there are other file writing locations with similar issues

**Confidence:** High. The evidence directly supports the conclusion: the "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue, and the code clearly shows auto-detected encoding being used for writes.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Issue-First Hypothesizer and Minimal Diff Finder on the root cause and location. Their analysis matches my findings exactly.
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater encoding flow is relevant but secondary - even if the dbt templater handles encoding correctly, the core issue is in `linted_file.py` using auto-detected encoding for writes.
- I believe the fix should be at the write point (always use UTF-8) rather than improving detection, as UTF-8 is the safest write encoding since it supports all Unicode characters.

### Minimal Diff Finder — Round 2

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method uses auto-detected file encoding (via chardet) when writing files, which may return a limited encoding like Windows-1252 that cannot encode Unicode characters like '→', causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated because Python's `open()` with mode "w" truncates immediately.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Change the file write operation in `persist_tree` to always use UTF-8 encoding (which supports all Unicode characters), regardless of what encoding was auto-detected for reading. This is a 1-line change from `encoding=self.encoding` to `encoding="utf-8"`.

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` confirms Windows-1252 encoding is being used for writing
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` from auto-detection via `get_encoding()` in `file_helpers.py`
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails, the file remains empty
4. The `get_encoding()` function uses chardet which can misidentify UTF-8 files with limited character samples as ASCII or Windows-1252

**Weaknesses:**
- I haven't verified if there are legitimate use cases requiring preservation of original file encoding
- There may be other file writing locations with similar issues I didn't check
- The dbt templater path may have additional encoding complications

**Confidence:** High. The evidence directly supports the conclusion: the "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the code clearly shows auto-detected encoding being used for writes where UTF-8 should be used.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with all three investigators on the root cause and location. The analysis is consistent across all perspectives.
- The Issue-First Hypothesizer and Minimal Diff Finder correctly identified that the fix should be at the write point (use UTF-8) rather than improving detection.
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater encoding flow is relevant but secondary - the core issue is in `linted_file.py` using auto-detected encoding for writes, which is the minimal fix location.

### Issue-First Hypothesizer — Round 1

 **Root cause:** The file writing in `persist_tree` uses an auto-detected encoding that may not support Unicode characters (e.g., 'charmap'/cp1252 on Windows), causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated/wiped because Python's `open()` with 'w' mode truncates the file before writing.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Ensure files are always written with UTF-8 encoding (which supports all Unicode characters), regardless of what encoding was detected for reading. Alternatively, catch encoding errors during write and retry with UTF-8, or use a safer write pattern (write to temp file, then rename).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error message `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` indicates the file is being written with Windows-1252 (charmap) encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` which comes from `get_encoding()` auto-detection
3. The file gets wiped because Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately, and if the `f.write()` fails due to encoding error, the file remains empty
4. The dbt templater passes encoding through the chain, but the core issue is the write encoding doesn't match the content's actual character set

**Weaknesses:**
- I didn't find existing tests for Unicode handling in the fix path
- The exact path from dbt templater through to persist_tree could have other encoding handoff issues I didn't fully trace
- There may be legitimate reasons to preserve the original file encoding that I'm not considering

**Confidence:** High. The 'charmap' codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue when using 'w' mode.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Stack Trace Tracer's focus on encoding detection, but I believe the fix should be at the write point (always use UTF-8 for writing) rather than improving detection
- The issue is not just detection but the mismatch between detected encoding and the actual character content - UTF-8 is the safest write encoding since it supports all Unicode characters
- The file wiping is a secondary effect of the encoding error during write, not a separate bug

---

### Minimal Diff Finder — Round 1

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method in `linted_file.py` opens the file with mode "w" (which truncates immediately) and uses an auto-detected encoding that may not support Unicode characters, causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file empty when the write fails.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding

**Proposed fix direction:** Change the file write operation to use UTF-8 encoding explicitly (since it supports all Unicode characters), or implement atomic file writing (write to a temporary file first, then rename on success).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` indicates the write is using Windows-1252 encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` from auto-detection
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails, the file remains empty
4. The `get_encoding()` function in `file_helpers.py` uses chardet which may return "ascii" or "windows-1252" for files with limited character samples, even if they contain UTF-8 characters

**Weaknesses:**
- I haven't verified if there are legitimate use cases where preserving the original file encoding is important
- The dbt templater path may have additional encoding complications I didn't fully trace
- There may be other places in the codebase with similar file writing patterns

**Confidence:** High. The "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Issue-First Hypothesizer's analysis of the root cause and location
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater is relevant but secondary - the core issue is in the file writing logic in `linted_file.py`
- The minimal fix is to use UTF-8 for writing, which will handle all Unicode characters correctly regardless of what encoding was detected for reading

### Issue-First Hypothesizer — Round 2

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method in `linted_file.py` uses an auto-detected file encoding (via chardet) when writing files, which may return a limited encoding like Windows-1252 ("charmap") that cannot encode Unicode characters like '→', causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated/wiped because Python's `open()` with mode "w" truncates the file before writing.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function that auto-detects encoding using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Always use UTF-8 encoding when writing files in `persist_tree`, since UTF-8 supports all Unicode characters. The encoding used for reading can remain auto-detected, but writing should use a superset encoding (UTF-8) to ensure all characters can be persisted safely. Alternatively, implement atomic file writing (write to temp file, then rename on success).

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error message `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` confirms the write is using Windows-1252 encoding which doesn't support the → character
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` which originates from `get_encoding()` auto-detection in `file_helpers.py`
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails with an encoding error, the file remains empty (explaining the "wiped" file symptom)
4. The `get_encoding()` function uses chardet which can return "ascii" or "windows-1252" for files with limited character samples, even if they contain UTF-8 characters

**Weaknesses:**
- I didn't trace the complete encoding flow through the dbt templater to confirm there aren't additional encoding issues there
- There may be legitimate use cases where preserving the original file encoding is important (though UTF-8 is a superset of ASCII and most common encodings)
- I haven't verified if there are other file writing locations with similar issues

**Confidence:** High. The evidence directly supports the conclusion: the "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, the file truncation behavior is a well-known Python file I/O issue, and the code clearly shows auto-detected encoding being used for writes.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with the Issue-First Hypothesizer and Minimal Diff Finder on the root cause and location. Their analysis matches my findings exactly.
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater encoding flow is relevant but secondary - even if the dbt templater handles encoding correctly, the core issue is in `linted_file.py` using auto-detected encoding for writes.
- I believe the fix should be at the write point (always use UTF-8) rather than improving detection, as UTF-8 is the safest write encoding since it supports all Unicode characters.

### Minimal Diff Finder — Round 2

 **Root cause:** The `persist_tree` method uses auto-detected file encoding (via chardet) when writing files, which may return a limited encoding like Windows-1252 that cannot encode Unicode characters like '→', causing a `UnicodeEncodeError` that leaves the file truncated because Python's `open()` with mode "w" truncates immediately.

**Location:** 
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/linter/linted_file.py` line 497: `with open(fname, "w", encoding=self.encoding) as f:`
- `/testbed/src/sqlfluff/core/file_helpers.py`: `get_encoding()` function using chardet

**Proposed fix direction:** Change the file write operation in `persist_tree` to always use UTF-8 encoding (which supports all Unicode characters), regardless of what encoding was auto-detected for reading. This is a 1-line change from `encoding=self.encoding` to `encoding="utf-8"`.

**Supporting evidence:**
1. The error `'charmap' codec can't encode character '\u2192'` confirms Windows-1252 encoding is being used for writing
2. In `linted_file.py` line 497, `persist_tree` uses `self.encoding` from auto-detection via `get_encoding()` in `file_helpers.py`
3. Python's `open(fname, "w", ...)` truncates the file immediately upon opening, so if `f.write()` fails, the file remains empty
4. The `get_encoding()` function uses chardet which can misidentify UTF-8 files with limited character samples as ASCII or Windows-1252

**Weaknesses:**
- I haven't verified if there are legitimate use cases requiring preservation of original file encoding
- There may be other file writing locations with similar issues I didn't check
- The dbt templater path may have additional encoding complications

**Confidence:** High. The evidence directly supports the conclusion: the "charmap" codec error is a classic Windows encoding issue, and the code clearly shows auto-detected encoding being used for writes where UTF-8 should be used.

**Reactions:**
- I agree with all three investigators on the root cause and location. The analysis is consistent across all perspectives.
- The Issue-First Hypothesizer and Minimal Diff Finder correctly identified that the fix should be at the write point (use UTF-8) rather than improving detection.
- The Stack Trace Tracer's focus on the dbt templater encoding flow is relevant but secondary - the core issue is in `linted_file.py` using auto-detected encoding for writes, which is the minimal fix location.
